ParlaMint-DK_20160503101308	As the previous rapporteur mentioned, the draft law here is about repealing the so-called 60-40 scheme on aid to solar cells. This is a quite violent process, especially given that only 2.6 MW of installations have been approved so far, i.e. a two thousand doubling of the scheme in a very short time if all those who have applied for aid are granted. If the aid is granted, it is equivalent to the grant of PSO aid of approx. 1.4 billion kr. a year in the first year of the support period, and over the entire period of 10 years we will reach a total of 11 billion. kr. In other words, it is a huge bill that the Danes, Danish electricity consumers and Danish companies will pay if this scheme is allowed to continue. Another dimension of the consequences of allowing the scheme to continue is that it will be a quite considerable part of Denmark's area that will be patched up with solar cells. In various media today, I have seen the Minister quote that these solar cells will fill more than the entire area of Samsø. I can also figure out that my own municipality, Hvidovre, will be patched up with solar cells several times if these applications are allowed to be met. So you have to ask yourself whether that is what Denmark should look like: solar cells all over the place? I have to say that the answer from the Danish People's Party is no. We have been warning for a long time that the PSO support schemes are very expensive, and here we see another example of the fact that if you do not fence those schemes well and thoroughly, things can get out of hand, and that is what has been happening here. Therefore, it is probably no surprise that the Danish People's Party supports the bill here for the abolition of the so-called 60-40 scheme.

ParlaMint-DK_20160503104337	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then I give the floor to the Minister for Energy, Supply and Climate.
ParlaMint-DK_20160503104345	Thank you for that, President. Let me begin by thanking the rapporteurs for their comments on the draft law and also for the positive reception by the vast majority of parties in the Folketing. As has been shown, the purpose of the draft law is to abolish the 60-40 aid scheme for solar installations. An exorbitant number of applications for commitments for the 60-40 aid scheme has been received. On Friday evening April 29th there were applications for a total of approx. 4,500 MW. Applications of approx. 4,000 MW. By comparison, capacity equivalent to 2.6 MW was installed on the scheme at the beginning of April. Furthermore, in our policy agreement on the solar PV system from 2013, we have provided for an extension of the scheme to a total of 60 MW by 2020. Energinet.dk has processed the applications as they have been received. This means that the PRS tax potentially increases significantly for each day that can be committed under the scheme. I therefore hope for your goodwill and understanding that it is essential that this bill be adopted today. Indeed, this would lead to the termination of the 60-40 aid scheme in order to ensure that no aid is granted for the continued expansion of solar installations under the 60-40 aid scheme. I would also like to thank the parties for their support for dispensing with the Rules of Procedure of the Danish Parliament. I have given priority to urgent consideration of the legislative proposal so that we can quickly and effectively get ahead of developments in the cost of solar energy expansion that is developing too rapidly. We are therefore taking action against an aid scheme which has developed in an entirely unforeseen direction. In the future, I will look into whether and how we can prevent a similar situation from occurring. Then a few questions have been asked to me. I would like to assure Martin Lidegaard, Pia Olsen Dyhr and Mette Abildgaard that we will sit down and look at how we deal with this in the future. We are going to deal with the fact that, with the commitments that have been made, we are already above the framework set for 2020 compared to the 60 MW that was provided. With the number of applications promised on Friday afternoon, we are over 60 MW. It is not just applications but commitments that have been made, and it is clear, of course, that it is not certain that all commitments mean that a PV plant is being built. And if we do not reach the 60 MW, let us sit down and discuss it. Unfortunately, however, I believe that I must inform the Danish Parliament that the challenge will probably be different, namely that we will instead sit down and discuss how the extra cost that has come will be dealt with, because there are likely to be support commitments for more than 60 MW, and that will be dealt with. ParlaMint-DK_20160503145712	Thank you. I would like to explain why the Danish People's Party has tabled amendments to the motion for a resolution on the suspensive effect on people with disabilities. With the motion for a resolution, the Danish People's Party wants suspensive effect on people with disabilities who had not improved their ability to function so that they did not suddenly have no help in their daily lives. A complaint may extend over several years, when the citizen will have to cope without the assistance and support he or she has been denied. Several citizens and a number of disability organisations and the Council of Lawyers have repeatedly pointed out the problem. The everyday life of people with disabilities is made more difficult during the period in which they are unduly deprived of the help that helps to maintain their normal way of life. This can affect their quality of life, impair their ability to function, impair their job situation and affect family life and affect them severely mentally all conditions which can cause irreparable damage. It was a wish of several parties to clarify the target group for suspensive effect, since it was not considered clear enough in the resolution. In order to obtain 90 mandates for this resolution, we have therefore accepted and clarified the wishes. It is a great day today, because we are going to help a great many people so that they are not taken hostage in a lengthy process of complaint. I can say a lot about the process in this process, but I will not. Instead, I would like to thank the parties that support and those who have supported all the way through. I would also like to thank the Minister and the Ministry, who have helped in drafting the amendments. It is then that we want to shout loudly, but out of respect for the President, I shall refrain from doing so, but I welcome all the positive feedback we have received on the motion for a resolution. The Multiple Sclerosis Society has written, and I quote: "Now we are finally getting the right focus on the fact that it cannot be the individual person who has to pay a high price for the system's shortcomings. ▪ And the Muscleswind Foundation writes that it is epoch-making that the Danish Parliament is finally thinking in those lines.

ParlaMint-DK_20160503151213	Does anyone wish to speak? Sir. Peder Hvelplund.
ParlaMint-DK_20160503151229	L 143 calls for the abolition of the differentiated treatment guarantee, which should instead be replaced by a general one. This means that there is a health assessment, which is replaced by an economic priority, and that the role of private hospitals in health care is increased. We in the Enhedslist support, of course, the right to rapid investigation and treatment, but this is not achieved by a guarantee which the proposal here proposes. That's what you get by getting more specialists. There is today, for example. lack of specialists in rheumatology, in children's psychiatry and in neurology. This issue is just getting worse with L 143. Instead of having resources moved from the specialties where competition is taking place, the proposal will give the regions priority, for resource reasons, operations such as back, hip, knee and obesity operations. Instead, there is a risk of downgrading medical patients with multiple diagnoses, of the harder psychiatric patients and of the acute patients today accounting for 75%. of all patients. Furthermore, the proposal means that we will have an even more fragmented health care system. Patients do not need more courses in different hospitals. Instead, there is a need for a coherent patient course where there is close cooperation between the different units. The unitary list sees it as a step backwards when abolishing the differentiated treatment guarantee of 30 days for life-threatening diseases and replacing it with a general one. As a consequence, some patients with relatively lighter genes are pushed forward in line in front of patients with more complicated courses. The proposal therefore does not, overall, contribute to improving health care. This alone contributes to the strengthening of the private sector in health care. This is something we have previously agreed with the Socialists on, but unfortunately the Socialists have now changed their position. That is why we have tabled an amendment so that we maintain the assurance of clarification, but so that the differentiated treatment guarantee is maintained. It is a proposal which has been supported by the Regional Council Presidents both in the capital and in North Jutland, and it is still my hope that the Socialists will vote in favour of that amendment so that we can have a united opposition to make the call for a rapid investigation, but reject the support for private hospitals.

ParlaMint-DK_20160503151813	The debate is open. Sir. Flemming Møller Mortensen, go ahead.
ParlaMint-DK_20160503151925	Thank you, President. There are many people interested in this health bill in the Chamber, I hear it's nice. I have to say right away that the Socialists support this bill. There is nothing more important for us health politicians than patients' safety, and that is exactly what this bill is about. It is with great pleasure that we have arrived at a first-reading legislative proposal. In fact, over the last three years we have been working very closely on this issue and the challenges posed by the supervision of health professionals and health institutions. We have to go back to the spring of 2013, when Denmark's Radio brought a documentary about a doctor, that is, a medical professional who did not comply with what was good medical practice. At that time we had a Minister for Health, Mrs Astrid Krag, who started a coal excavation, in order to find out what the conditions in the Danish Health Authority and in Danish practice are actually. An expert opinion was obtained from an association of supervisors in Europe in order to obtain an independent assessment of a coal excavation of the Danish conditions. They made 57 recommendations regarding the supervision of doctors and other healthcare professionals and recommended and specified where improvements could be made. Based on this, the Danish Health Authority prepared an action plan with different areas of action. And then what happens? Then there will be a very, very critical relationship with a health professional. It was also Denmark's Radio that uncovered problem fields here. This meant that we actually made a very broad political agreement that the Danish Health Authority should have provided more resources and that some independent studies should be carried out in certain areas. Then we are moving towards a change of government in which the Minister for Health is working on the tactics of the Social Democratic-led government to say how we could build a health services company in a different, more efficient, more quality way. And this is where we are with regard to the proposed legislative proposal, which means that we are changing our supervisory practices from being highly reactive, a little routine, to being proactive, with a risk assessment of the various areas of health and health care, to setting up a new and better supervisory function. There's been a change in the structure of the Danish Health Authority, so we've also got a management board for patient safety, which is supposed to be in charge of this field. That is very good, and it is very welcome, because nothing is more important for a health service than proper and thorough supervision. Why? Yes, because patients, citizens, but also all those who work in the field of health need to know that things are being followed up, that things are being watched and, of course, that the necessary sanctions are being imposed if something has not happened, as it should be. In addition to these amendments, there are other elements in the draft law, and let me just mention three of them. There will be a look at the possibility of also depriving Danish health professionals of the authorisation if other countries in the Nordic countries have withdrawn the authorisation. It is also based on several bad stories that have affected patients here or in other Nordic countries, where health professionals, as is popularly said, shop from one country to another and produce a practice that is not in order according to good practice. Another element of the law is that some of the rules that are in this area are being cleaned up, and this is all helping to make the field more clear. The last thing I should like to mention is that in the field of medicine, it is intended to make a better, sensible and thorough exchange of information and data across the board, all of which will help to raise the picture of medicine consumption in Denmark, but also to bring out information that can help improve patient safety. It is a good bill, the economy is linked, and we have been negotiating politically very broadly over the last three years, and we are almost in line with the fact that all parties are involved. I am sorry that the Radical Left cannot support this bill, because it would have been strong for all parties to be involved. Nordic co-operation has also been strengthened, just as politically there is also a great deal of attention to the fact that European cooperation with regard to supervision and authorisation must also be an important part of the future. The Social Democrats are happy to support this bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20160503153043	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments, so we go further in the rapporteur's report to Mrs Jane Heitmann, Left.
ParlaMint-DK_20160503153050	Thank you for the floor. The draft law on future supervision has many focal points and is probably what one might call a large overall package. In addition to creating a new supervisory framework, the term 'official doctor' and 'official medical institution' shall be deleted. This is not because there are to be changes in the distribution of tasks between the two decentralised sites west of Storebælt, but to clarify that the three units within the Patient Safety Management Board are part of a single organisational and task portfolio. I have chosen in my rapporteur's speech to focus on a few key points, and that does not mean that the other elements of the draft law are not important. It is merely an expression of the fact that speaking time in this platform is limited. One of the elements of the bill is to create a legislative legal basis for a completely new proactive, reactive and risk-based supervision of treatment sites and nursing homes. Another element is the creation of a strict set of rules for the withdrawal of authorisations and the restriction of the practice of authorised health professionals. The draft law is a consequence of the policy agreement on risk-based supervision of treatment sites, which was agreed on 11 February 2016. All parties support the agreement, except the Radical Left. The broad political support is a great strength and sends a signal that patient safety is high among the parties. The new risk-based supervision will reinforce the focus on those areas where there is a high risk to patient safety and where the consequences for errors can be fatal to the patient. I would like to acknowledge that the previous government took the initiative to initiate work in order to create health and safety. For us in the Left, patient safety and security are essential, because we have heard too many sad stories where it has been clear to everyone that the system has failed. In recent years we have been able to follow stories in the press, for example. on a psychiatrist from West Jutland who seems to have mistreated patients who have approached in good faith for treatment for serious mental disorders. On Funen we have been able to follow through the press a story about a psychiatrist who apparently has also mistreated his patients. Common to the stories is that Danish patients have often been treated with wrong preparations or excessive doses, and this has had fatal consequences for some of the patients. There have been several concerns regarding doctors to the Danish Health Service, which have not been responded to, and when the cases were finally dealt with, the damage had been done. And that's not good enough, so it's time to tighten up. The Minister for Health and the Elderly has previously led an organisational reorganisation of the new board, the Board for Patient Safety, which is to be the frontrunner. Now we have a legal basis in place so that organization and legal bases can go hand in hand. In the Nordic countries, we have strong traditions for doctors, such as is authorised in Denmark, has easily been able to obtain authorisation in Norway, and it is undoubtedly a good thing that authorized doctors in the Nordic countries can work freely. Unfortunately, we have been able to follow sad cases, including the press. a Danish doctor has worked both in Denmark and in Norway and in Sweden, and in all three countries a number of patients, relatives and doctors have complained about his treatments, just as last year a case pops up in the press where a Danish psychiatrist had been deprived of an authorisation in Norway after a sexual assault on a patient, but could continue as a psychiatrist in Denmark. For us in the Left, it is crucial that the Board for Patient Safety now has the opportunity to respond quickly in those cases and to assess whether a doctor is a danger to Danish patients and with the law in hand could deprive a given health care professional of the authorisation immediately, although there is of course also a legal certainty to go to the health care professional. Thousands of authorized health professionals carry out daily tasks with great professionalism for Danish patients. As an authorised health care professional, you take a great deal of responsibility, and it is important to stress that from my chair we all have an interest in strong and effective supervision in Denmark, whether you are a patient or a treatment. Unfortunately, the introduction of risk-based supervision is not free of charge. In order to ensure a fair distribution of the costs of the new supervision, there is political agreement among the parties to the agreement on a fee financing on the basis of a self-sustaining principle. It is graduated in such a way that the largest units pay the most and the smallest. The consultation answers show, among other things, that some associations, for example: Danish Foot therapists believe that it will hit the small units hard. The fee for a single-person clinic with a foottherapist is set at 1.891 DKK. annual. In evaluating the legislative proposal, I think it would be quite natural that there should also be an evaluation of the fee structure. In addition to being a health rapporteur, I am also an older rapporteur, and I have read with interest the consultation responses from Danish Seniors Councils and the Elders Case and Alzheimer's Association. From the outset, I understand the concern expressed in the transition from frequency-based to risk-based supervision, as well as the impatience of associations with the implementation of permanent care homes. I also think that this should be done today rather than tomorrow. Residential home residents are a particularly weak and exposed group, which requires us to keep a sharp focus on the conditions. In the Left, we will follow the area closely, and although the municipalities have an independent supervisory obligation, which I naturally expect to be met, I will read with great attention the evaluations in the area of nursing homes when they are available in approx. 3 years. On the left side, we can back the bill.

Of course, we support this in the Enhedslisten, also because it is being established that there is still the possibility of early supervision being carried out. However, we have a number of concerns about the draft law, which we would like to look at a little more closely in committee. On the one hand, we are a little concerned about the availability of the resources to carry out qualified supervision. On the one hand, we are concerned about the disappearance of the quality development resulting from more continuous supervision. After all, supervision is not just a control of possible failures. After all, supervision is also an opportunity to get some sparring in relation to quality development, to provide advice and guidance and to make some exchange of experience in the whole area. And we may be concerned about the disappearance of that function with risk-based supervision. Some of this will, of course, be remedied if, for example, there is to be an end to this. are permanently affiliated doctors at the care centres. But there is also another part of the matter of time limits, and that is that it is said that there must be a processing period of up to 2 months in cases where a health care professional is suspected and therefore temporarily has his or her authorisation withdrawn. Some people have said in the hearings that this is a long time, and I can follow them in that way. It is clear that this is, of course, a balance between patient safety and care for the caregivers. But we will then try to find out in the committee's work whether it is not possible to set clearer deadlines in order to ensure a certain level of safety for both employees and patients. We also find it regrettable that official medical services should be abolished. We think it is important to maintain the respect and autonomy of the institution. In fact, we do not think that it is only about the word that it is important to maintain the function of the medical institution. As far as funding is concerned, we are pleased that the government will compensate the municipalities and regions for the additional costs they will incur in connection with the fee for risk-based supervision. It will, of course, be a form of general compensation, and that is why we would also like to pursue in committee whether there could be any burdensome imbalance in the distribution of expenditure, in other words ensuring that the compensation will also come back to the institutions that will pay for it. Finally, there is the whole question of data information. There are some considerations to take into account both patient safety and the possibility of detecting structural errors, including with a long time horizon. At the same time, there is also concern for data security and the right of citizens to know what information is being shared and with whom. It is essential to ensure that it is quite clear who has access to which data. The greater a database is built and the more accessable it is, the greater the risk of accidental use. But we would also like to pursue this in the work of the committee. So, despite the fact that we have a number of concerns, we generally take a very positive view of this and look forward to further work on the proposal in committee.

ParlaMint-DK_20160503154112	Thank you. For a period of time, unfortunately, we have seen several examples of gross failure by health professionals, and in order to minimise this in the future, all parties have unfortunately concluded a political agreement, with the exception of the Radical Left, which is the basis for this bill. The aim of the bill is to strengthen patient safety in Denmark, which the Liberal Alliance considers very important, and that is why we support the proposal. The proposal is quite comprehensive, in fact it is 173 pages, but there are two elements in particular which are of great importance for increased patient safety. Monitoring is transformed into risk-based, where it is currently frequency-based. The bill will organise the visits so that they reflect the risks, so focus on the treatment sites that pose the greatest risk to the patient. This reduces the risk of patients being exposed to treatment that does not meet safety and quality requirements. The treatment centres are divided into five categories, which pay differentiated fees, depending on the size and number of doctors. The cost structure has been a point in the agreement that the Liberal Alliance has not entirely agreed with. We believe that it should have been possible to apply supervision to the current budget and at no extra cost to business of 17.3 million. kr. By focusing on the most risky treatment sites, this should be an optimisation of the supervision organisation and thus the possibility of improvement within the current framework. We are particularly sorry that small single-member companies, such as the United Kingdom, are not in a position to do so. Fed therapists are also charged a fee, and even though during the negotiations it came down to 1.891 DKK. a year, I still think it shouldn't have been there. Another point that is good for everything is that the rules on the withdrawal of authorisation are being tightened up. It is also very important, given the terrible things that have been going on. From now on, a health care professional will automatically be deprived of his authorisation in Denmark if it is taken away abroad, with the exception that the Liberal Alliance is very concerned that the withdrawal cannot take place if it is evident that the reason for the foreign withdrawal would not have led to the withdrawal of authorisation in Denmark. All in all, this bill is of great importance for improving patient safety in Denmark, so although we in the Liberal Alliance are opposed to the fee structure, we believe that patient safety is far above that and we therefore support the proposal. There have been some good and constructive negotiations, and the result is an agreement and a bill that will have a major impact on improving patient safety in Denmark.

But I must also say frankly that we have had many speculations along the way. But it's not really because we disagree with the king's idea behind it, namely the shift to this risk-based supervision. Of course, it makes good sense that instead of having these regulatory frequency-based supervisions, which are being carried out today on, for example, nursing homes, make it more risk-based. In other words, you put all the data, and that is for example. Causes of concern, etc., into a large engine and then on the basis of it gets the best idea of where to target your supervision. That makes a lot of sense in all kinds of ways. But there have still been many elements we have asked critically about in this process. It has been a long process; it has taken three years, as rapporteur for the Socialist Group, Mr President. Flemming Møller Mortensen, pointed out. I haven't had the pleasure of joining for that long, but I could tell from my colleagues in here that it was almost quite historic when you got this agreement. Compared to these charges, we've asked a lot about the way it's categorized. We have had difficulty in understanding that there should be no greater difference in what it costs to supervise a nursing home, where everything else is equal to many employees, and then what it costs to supervise, for example, a practicing foot therapist who has his own small clinic. We have asked a lot about this, and we also made concessions in the process, and for that we are very grateful. An independent category has been established now that some of these players, which we do not consider to be the very high risk, have been introduced, i.e. the therapists for example. We have asked a lot about whether there are any figures or other evidence that they should be subject to this supervision, but since they have not been supervised in the past, it is by nature difficult to assess the risk. This can be done a little on the basis of figures from the Patient Replacement, etc., but it does not give a comprehensive picture. We have now got them into an independent category, and then we look forward very much to the law being evaluated as 3 years. We will actually have figures on whether there was anything to be done with, for example, these therapists, physiotherapists and so on, and on the basis of this we can assess whether we had the right arrangement of these categories and of the fees in three years' time. We also in the Ministry asked incredibly much whether it was really what it cost to do a supervision, i.e. this $1,891. for the lowest category. And I've been confirmed several times that this rests in itself. No more money is required for supervision than it costs to carry out individual supervision. That is indisputable, as it were, and we have to believe that when it is the assessment that has been made. We look forward to the evaluation and we also look forward to seeing how this agreement will come about, for example in the regions. Will general practice require them to cover this new fee they have to pay? For then there will of course be an expense elsewhere in the system, in addition to the funds we have found in the conclusion of this agreement. So all in all, we are happy and satisfied with the agreement, which we think is a step in the right direction, although we have had objections along the way in the process and so still have things we might wish for differently. But that's the way it is when broad agreements are made, which is what this is all about.
ParlaMint-DK_20160503155756	We thank the rapporteurs. Then it's the Minister of Health and the Elders.
ParlaMint-DK_20160503155806	Thank you for that, and thank you to the rapporteurs for receiving the bill and the debate here today. Patient safety is an important priority for the government and I am pleased to be able to listen to and also note through the many good discussions we have had along the way that patient safety is something that concerns us all. So thank you for all the statements that have been made today and for the progress we have had, which I also think has been very constructive and which was about finding solutions for the benefit of patient safety. I note with satisfaction that there is broad support for us to provide the Patient Safety Agency with the right tools to carry out effective and targeted supervision for the benefit of patients with the bill here. I have also noted that the consultations we have received from the external consultation on the legislative proposal are generally positive. Unfortunately, we are all aware of the cases that have been raised in relation to the unsatisfactory handling of supervision by the former administration of specific health professionals. There were cases where the Board simply took too long to take action, just as there were cases of maladministration. Therefore, back in May 2015, we reached a broad political agreement on the organisation of future supervision. With the agreement, we took some of the first important steps on the road to better and more efficient supervision. Among other things: We promised each other to explore the possibility of making the Patient Safety Management Board's supervision of treatment sites more risk-based. We had a series of negotiations on this at the beginning of this year, and this resulted in a largely unanimous Parliament taking responsibility for redeploying the supervision of treatment sites. In this way, we can take action where the risk to patient safety is greatest. It is still a mystery to me why a party such as the Radicals chose to be outside that agreement, and I have to say that I did not become much wiser when I listened to the rapporteur's speech by the radical rapporteur. There was something about some re-priority contribution that was supposed to be responsible for getting people fired in the health care system. I mean, this government has raised the health and elderly area by 3.4 billion. kr. This is considerably more than what the radical government partner proposed when it was recently held responsible, and I think the criticism sounds rather hollow. Today Radicals are so opposed to the bill because of something about the fact that no more people are to be recruited in the Board of Patient Safety. That is a strange argument in itself. You want better supervision, but you do not want to hire someone to carry out the task you would like to have done. It ignores the fact that today we have supervision, in which we sort of close our eyes to large parts of the treatment centres, which carry out tasks in our health care, and then we focus very narrowly and very zealously on a tiny part of it. I wonder why you don't want to take responsibility for that. With this bill here, I will implement our agreement to make supervision more risk-based. I will come back to that because the draft law contains more and more. The Patient Safety Management Board must have better opportunities to intervene. The many initiatives reassure me that we are now cleaning up the store a little, and that we are on the right path to provide the Patient Safety Agency with the tools necessary to be able to take rapid action against those healthcare providers who are not aware of their responsibilities. I would like to make a few comments on the individual parts of the bill, and I would like to take first the supervision of treatment sites: action must be taken here where there are most risks to patient safety. That is why we propose to shift to risk-based supervision. It is intended that the Patient Safety Management Board can focus resources on the basis of other relevant data and take action where there is the greatest risk to patient safety. The risk-based supervision complements the Board's general reactive supervision of treatment sites. This means that the Board must continue to respond to all treatment sites where there is a concrete suspicion of a risk to patient safety. However, with a shift in supervision towards being more risk-based, we ensure that the Agency uses resources where the need is greatest. At the same time, we ensure that forces are not tied up to regular supervision of those places where there is not necessarily a risk to patient safety. As the rapporteur for the Alternative also indicated, this can be seen as a joint expression on the part of the Danish Parliament about a greater degree of confidence in our many skilled staff in our common health care system. It is about using the forces where there is the greatest danger to patient safety, and then having increased confidence in those who lift their work really well every day. On the other hand, the publication system, as the rapporteur for the Alternative also mentioned, is not about trust. It is about openness, that is to say, that our patients must have access to openness and transparency about decisions, which are, of course, criticism of serious or repeated negligence. I don't see that information we have to hide from anyone, let alone the patients. So it has nothing to do with trust. This has something to do with signaling openness to those for whom our health services are intended, namely patients. In the view of the government, effective supervision is also conditional upon the Patient Safety Agency having the right tools to be able to take rapid action against health professionals who mistreat and endanger patients. That is why I propose that the rules be tightened so that the Patient Safety Agency will have a better chance of stopping dangerous health professionals. When we tighten the rules, it is also my concern to ensure the legal certainty of health professionals at the same time. Therefore, it is no less important now that the board complies with the administrative rules of the game and deals with matters as quickly as possible. On this point, I believe that we have actually listened to the answers received and have found a reasonable balance in relation to the draft law and its comments. This is, of course, also an issue which I would like to discuss further with the members of the committee in the further consideration of the draft law. As part of the tightening, I suggest that the Patient Safety Management Board get some brand new tools in their range of response options. This concerns, among other things: on the possibility of suspending an authorisation and imposing a ban on professional activities while a case is being examined. As another essential element, I propose that health care professionals should automatically be deprived of or restricted from their Danish authorisation if they have had the right to practise the profession covered by the Danish authorisation curtailed or deprived abroad. The fact that doctors and other health professionals who may be at risk for patients can simply stop travelling from one country to another and treat patients without the supervisory authorities being able to intervene. I also propose a simplification and clean-up of the supervisory rules, so that we have simple and transparent rules for the benefit of both the supervisory authorities and the health care professionals, and I also propose an extension of the Health Data Protection Agency's access to pass on personal pharmaceutical information from the Pharmaceutical Administration Register. I do, among other things. to ensure that the Patient Safety Management Board can obtain the necessary information for their supervision. That is, for example, the case. in situations where the medical record is incomplete or even misleading. On this point too, therefore, the proposal will benefit patient safety. Finally, I would also like to mention that we are proposing a number of other relevant amendments with the bill. Among other things: legislation will reflect that last autumn I took the initiative to set up the Board of Patient Safety as a new independent board. The Patient Safety Management Board has taken over oversight tasks from the then Health Board and the treatment of patient complaints from the Patient Offers. In addition, it is proposed that the term 'official doctors' should be deleted. That was a great deal, I recall, of a completely different draft law from the previous government, and perhaps we need to have a better discussion of the relationship between it and all the other things that needed to be changed in relation to supervision. We will get it done now, but it will not affect the tasks that the Patient Safety Agency will have to perform. The administration will continue to have two decentralised units in Jutland, including: carry out supervisory tasks. With the bill, we are now setting an ambitious framework for future supervision. Now action has been taken behind the words, and with the bill I present a comprehensive package, the overall aim of which is to strengthen patient safety and ensure effective supervision, which can quickly address those healthcare professionals and healthcare providers who do not meet the requirements that can rightly be imposed on them. I am very pleased that it seems that there is broad support for the individual parts of the legislative proposal. I look forward to further discussions and, of course, make myself available for what may be of questions during committee reading. I have also noted that a number of issues have been raised here today, which the members of the committee would like to see clarified further, and we can, of course, continue to do so in committee. But thank you for the nice reception, not least for the good and constructive cooperation along the way towards the draft law's first reading here today.

ParlaMint-DK_20160503160922	The debate is open. The first to speak is the Minister for the Environment and Food. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160503160932	Thank you for that, President. Thank you to the Danish People's Party for tabling a motion for a resolution on the subject. First of all, I would like to make it clear that animals must, of course, be treated properly in terms of animal welfare and that the EU animal welfare legislation must, of course, be respected. At the same time, I recognise that killing by bullfighting is not illegal in all parts of Spain and that there is no EU legislation banning this type of killing either. In fact, the Treaty of Lisbon makes it clear that EU policies must weigh up the concerns of animal welfare on the one hand and respect for the cultural traditions and regional customs of the Member States on the other, which also means bullfighting. Next, let me give you a brief account of the problem raised by the proposal. First of all, it is important to state that the current EU agricultural policy does not provide direct financial support for the rearing of bulls for slaughter by bullfighting. As the authors of the proposal are also aware, the agricultural aid received by breeders of bull calves for bullfighting is indirect aid and is understood in the sense that the basic payment, i.e. the aid previously known as the aid per hectare, is granted to all types of agricultural area and therefore also to the areas covered by those bulls. Such indirect support is difficult to prevent. At one time, the EU's agricultural aid was conditional on or linked to the production of certain products, for example. meat cattle and cereals. Over the last 20 years, however, EU agricultural support has become increasingly decoupled and more and more market-oriented. Thus the aid is no longer linked to specific forms of production, but is given to all agricultural areas and production restrictions such as milk quotas have been abolished at the same time. This is a healthy development, which I believe all the parties in the Folketing support. It is a major step in the right direction that the EU's agricultural support is currently being given to all forms of agricultural activity. In this way farmers can adjust their own production in the light of market demand. It is difficult for me to see how it is possible to establish rules to ensure that the EU's agricultural aid, which, as I said, is granted to all types of agricultural land, is not also granted to producers who breed bulls, which may later be used for bullfighting, especially when the Treaty of Lisbon specifically mentions the consideration of cultural traditions and regional customs, as bullfighting is of course. I believe that it would be contrary to Denmark's general interests to work for such a change in the common agricultural policy. Over the past many years, changing governments have been working precisely for a continued decoupling and market orientation of agricultural aid. At the same time, Denmark risks losing influence in the forthcoming negotiations on the future of EU agricultural aid if the government should actively seek to introduce complex requirements as to which forms of agricultural production can be supported and which cannot, depending on what happens to the product after it leaves the farmer. For these reasons, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution, but I look forward to the committee reading, where I hope we can achieve a common understanding of this complex issue, and I would like to thank you once again for submitting the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160503165850	Thank you, sir. President. See, in the summer of 2014, I was in Barcelona. It was like a wonderful journey: nice weather, nice people and lovely food. We also had time for a small family trip in Las Arenas on Plaça d'Espanya. Had I visited this place in 1977, it would have been to watch one or more bulls live to enthusiasm and moro for thousands of Spaniards. Now Las Arenas is a shopping mall where you can enjoy yourself together in a completely different peaceful way. Since 2011 it has been prohibited to have bullfighting in Catalonia, and the Canary Islands have also adopted a ban, but unfortunately bullfighting is still a reality in many other parts of Spain. Animal protection associations estimate that at least 40,000 bulls die every year in bullrings around Spain. These are animals that are bred for the sole purpose of being killed to entertain people. And the bull doesn't stand a chance. Before a bullfight, the bull's horns are shredded so that it cannot defend itself properly against the matador in the ring. The bull is held in total darkness and is stabbed at the same time with spears to provoke it to display the sought-after aggressive behavior inside the arena. Probably inside the arena, the bull doesn't stand a chance. It's just about humiliating the animal and then killing it. It is a barbaric custom which does not belong anywhere in the 21st century, and it is a barbaric custom that Danish taxpayers help finance. Of course, not with our good will, but with the EU's aid per hectare, the rearing of bull calves is supported by more than 1 billion. kr. a year. According to the report "Toros & Taxes" it is also a support that helps to keep bullfighting alive. If the EU did not support the rearing of bull calves for bullfighters, the bullfighting industry would be on its way to rabunding. The European Parliament has twice, with the support of all Danish Members representing all parties in the European Parliament, adopted calls for the European Commission to ensure that EU funds are not used directly or indirectly to support bullfights or activities involving animal torture. This is among other things. After the discharge has been given to the EU's accounts. However, the EU's finance ministers have not followed up the resolution with action, and the European Parliament can only express Parliament's opinion, not impose Parliament's views. And in this case, it must be said to be a shame. More than 40,000 Danes signed a petition in 2015 to stop bullfighting. This appeal was listened to by the European Parliament, but those who can really do something are in the national parliaments and in the governments around the Member States. It is we here in the Folketing who equip the government with mandates for how to act in the European Union. We have the opportunity here today, and when the motion is put to the vote in Parliament, to send the clear message to the government that we want this kind of animal abuse stopped; that we do not help Denmark pay for animals to be killed for the sole purpose of entertaining people. I understand that the Minister is very difficult, among other things. because agricultural aid has been decoupled and released from production restrictions, as stated by Article 13 of the Treaty of Lisbon, because respect must be given to Member States in respect of religious rituals, cultural traditions or regional customs for which bullfighting is included. In other words, because the Spaniards have been tortured animals for years, we must accept it, and yes, we must not only accept it, we must also pay for it. I don't think the Danes understand that, and I don't think animal friends understand that. I have to say frankly: I think it is much more important to get rid of the aid for bullfighting than to avoid a debate on this in relation to the agricultural aid schemes. When it is the European Union that is really keeping the bullfighter industry alive, as the report mentioned earlier points out, the European Union also has a tremendous responsibility to do something about it. It may well be that you have to look at production restrictions, but it may also be that you have to attack them in a completely different way, for example. by looking at the directive on animal protection and whether it can really be true that certain animals are exempt from the rules on humane killing, just because they are part of, for example, humane killing. Bullfight, acting or something. But it is also living creatures that are involved, animals that lose their lives every day for the sole purpose of entertaining people. Denmark has informal cooperation in the EU with a number of other countries that are progressive in the field of animal welfare. You could, for example. As a government, go to these countries and ask whether we can do something about this together. But when I listen to the Minister today, it is as if it is not an option. In the European Parliament, it is quite easy to sit and vote for these things. This does not have much practical significance, because the Council can simply ignore the decisions and have done so. No, the litmus test comes to the Assembly Hall. Here it should be shown who really believes that the important thing is to get rid of the vile form of animal cruelty that bullfighting is an expression of, and who thinks that it is easier just not to tilt the boat. Therefore, on behalf of both the Danish People's Party and the animals, I should like to thank the Enhedslisten, the Alternative and the Socialist People's Party for their support for our proposal, and at the same time inform Mrs Zenia Stampe that the Radical Left also supports the proposal. Bullfighting appears for the first time in history in the Gilgamesh bag. During my trip to Barcelona 2 years ago, I drove past Barcelona's largest now happily abandoned bullfighterrena, La Monumental. At that time it was unused. ParlaMint-DK_20160503172124	A year ago we dealt with a bill on citizenship, L 188 called it, with 3,314 names on it. When there were more than 3,000 names on the bill at that time, it was due to the fact that the Enhedslisten, together with the Social Democrats, the Radicals and the SF, had relaxed the excessively strict rules in the so-called circular on naturalisation in 2013. It is the circular which lays down the rules on who is to be included in a bill on the notification of citizenship, who should have submitted their case to the Committee on the Rights of the Parliament and who should be refused. Then the many cases at L 188 are due, not least, to the fact that in 2013 Enhedslisten managed to press the then government to hire many more staff in the Ministry's birthright office so that it waited thousands of cases that were lying waiting to be dealt with could be settled. So the majority of the proceedings of 14 to 16 months, as notified to the applicants, did not go into the file but were in the waiting file. The basic attitude of the Enhedslisten was, and is, that 14-16 months are quite obscenely long, so we pressed the S-R-SF government to do something about the processing time, and the number of employees in the Ministry's birthright office increased from 25 in early 2013 to 85 2 years later. Now it's different times. Today we are discussing a bill on citizenship notification with 923 names on it. A year ago, it was 3.314. The only 923 names are primarily the result of the agreement reached by the Social Democrats and the Right in October 2015, which tightened the requirements for Danish citizenship. Since the 2015 elections, several have been refused because they do not comply with the new rules, and several have been refused because the majority of the Parliamentary Committee on the Rights of the Nation has voted against granting them an exemption, even if they had one or more long-term functional reductions, which have meant that there is one or more of the requirements that they do not meet. It has become harder to become a Danish citizen, and the group of people living in Denmark without Danish citizenship is growing. In 1979 it was 2%. of the population, today it is 8 per cent. A society in which a large group of citizens live without full civil rights is not linked. It is a completely distorted development that leads to the opposite of integration, the opposite of community. I would like to end with a congratulations. The vast majority of the 923 people in today's bill, namely 817 people, have had their cases dealt with in accordance with the new strict rules. They've slipped through a needle's eye that's gotten smaller. Congratulations. I should also like to welcome the Radicals and say that they also support the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20160503175014	Thank you to the rapporteur. We shall now proceed to the debate, and the first to speak is the Minister for Foreign Affairs, Integration and Housing. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160503175026	Thank you. First of all, I would like to thank the authors very much for this question about precisely as was also said the development of the proportion of the population with an immigrant background and its consequences, culturally and economically. The question is extremely relevant in view of the integration challenges that Danish society faces. The questioner refers to Denmark's Statistics population projection for 2015. This population projection will be the basis for my answer. The population projection shows that by 2050 there will be over 1.1 million immigrants or descendants in Denmark. This corresponds to just under one fifth of the population. Of these, two come out of three immigrants and descendants from non-western countries. Today there are more than 700,000 immigrants and descendants in Denmark. That's about 12%. of the population. However, it is important to distinguish between different types of immigrants. Many well-qualified immigrants work and earn money here in Denmark. They contribute to the Danish economy. But it is not good enough that only one in two immigrants from non-western countries are in employment today, compared with three out of four Danes. It is also true that immigrants who have lived in Denmark for 10 years and for some 20 years. And it is not good enough at all that only three out of ten refugees are in work after three years. It is therefore important that we make demands on refugees and immigrants. The requirement must be for all citizens in Denmark to be able to support themselves and also to be able to use the language, regardless of their background. Another important requirement must be that the refugees and immigrants who come here respect Danish fundamental values. These are values that mean that we in Denmark have complete equality between the sexes; that girls and boys, women and men are worth equally, that we live in a democracy and that we all enjoy the right to both think and speak freely. We can also draw what we want, regardless of political or religious beliefs. However, the common values are being challenged in these years when we are experiencing violent cultural clashes. I am aware that the best way to avoid cultural clashes is to ensure that the refugees and immigrants who come to Denmark get Denmark under the skin from day one. If you want to live in this country, that must mean putting medieval attitudes on, for example. equality on the shelf, no matter where you come from, no matter what your religion dictates. The government is therefore pursuing a consistent immigration policy to ensure that Denmark opens up to those who can and will and close the doors to those who do not want to. The Danish People's Party, the Liberal Alliance and the Conservative People's Party have, together with the Left, introduced the integration service, making it less attractive to come to Denmark. And this makes it more attractive to get a job and also much more attractive to learn the language. With the same parties, the Social Democratic Party has also made a number of other significant changes in the area of foreigners. Firstly, in November 2015, we adopted asylum package 1, which provides the authorities with a number of new tools to deal with an increase in the number of refugees and migrants entering and staying in Denmark and to ensure the maintenance of order. Thus, asylum package 1 introduced the possibility of derogating, inter alia, from the principle of equal treatment for men and women. in particular cases of asylum accommodation, to increase access to detention rejected asylum seekers, and at the same time to introduce shorter time limits for departure and a more consistent use of reporting and residence obligations. Secondly, in December, we adopted a legislative amendment allowing transporter responsibility to be implemented when travelling to Denmark from a Schengen country, if Denmark introduces temporary border controls. Thirdly, in January of this year we had asylum package 2 implemented, which contains some very significant restrictions in the area of asylum and migration, in order to make it significantly less attractive to come to Denmark. One of the elements of asylum package 2 is that access to family reunification for foreigners with temporary protection status is, as a rule, postponed for three years, including, as a starting point, the payment of a fee for, among other things, foreigners. the submission of applications for family reunification. In addition, the rules on obtaining a permanent residence permit have been tightened, which also has an indirect impact on the possibility of family reunification in Denmark. Asylum Package 2 also means that children's families in expulsion positions can no longer be accommodated in special accommodations associated with or outside asylum centres and that asylum seekers cannot move in self-employed accommodations associated with an asylum centre. In addition, the rules on the inclusion of refugee residence permits have been tightened and, in addition to those parts of asylum package 2 requiring legislative changes, the government has taken a number of initiatives that do not require amendment of the law. Thus, the government has implemented, among other things: a limitation of the duration of residence permits for refugees and the abolition of the extraordinary asylum advice. And since the government considers it reasonable that foreigners who want family reunification in Denmark themselves pay for the journey here, the system of State payments for the transport of family members to resident refugees has also been abolished. I believe that these austerity measures must have an impact on the number of foreigners who apply to Denmark, but, of course, the number is also linked to the situation we are in the world around us. At the same time, the government has tightened up the requirements for the acquisition of Danish citizenship. Thus, in the new agreement on citizenship of October, we have tightened the language requirements. We have tightened the rules on self-reliance and the waiting period for applicants who have committed criminal offences. Furthermore, the new agreement on citizenship means that in future applicants will have to document a knowledge of Danish social conditions, Danish culture and history with proof of having passed the new citizenship test. In addition, the agreement contains stricter requirements in relation to the content of medical certificates which may form the basis for exemption. Tightenings mean, inter alia, that in future applicants with mental disorders, including ptsd, must obtain a statement from a specialist in psychiatry or another person with a medical background based on prior psychiatric assessment of the patient. At the same time, the government will ensure better integration • integration, which means that much more refugees and immigrants will get to work and fewer people will live in parallel societies. Let me begin with some of our actions in the field of employment. We need to be much better at getting new refugees and families together more quickly into the companies, both because the refugees have to make a contribution to Danish society while they are here, but also because we know that it is absolutely crucial for the refugees' integration into Danish society that they enter the labour market where they meet Danes, Danish values and Danish languages. I would therefore like to acknowledge that the social partners, as well as the National Association of Local Authorities, have concluded two very ambitious agreements with the government, which make the integration effort much more employment-oriented. We have agreed that all new refugees and family members should meet as a job preparation, while at the same time the municipalities are obliged to give new refugees a business-oriented effort, at the very latest 1 month after they have arrived in a municipality. This will be of great benefit to the individual and to the local community. We have also agreed that Danish teaching should be more job-oriented, that language centres should be given stronger incentives to get refugees into business, and that education should be organised more flexibly so that it will never stand in the way of the student's ability to do a job. Finally, we propose that much more Danish education should take place in the workplace, where the companies themselves will be able to organise the teaching themselves. Too many immigrants and descendants live in ghetto areas today. This is unacceptable to the government, and it is unacceptable to Denmark, in my opinion. Denmark should not become a society in which people live divided according to their ethnic background or their religious background. Many bets are in progress, and it goes the right way in several places. Although estimates of ghetto areas from December show a decline from 31 to 25 ghetto areas, there are still major problems. We see new residential areas that turn into ghettos and other areas that do not move at all in relation to the parameters that we measure. That is why the government will draw up a plan to improve the social and physical efforts in the ghetto regions and break with the creepy trend of parallel societies, and unfortunately we see that in several places. We do not want closed ghetto areas, where the dishes turn away from Danish society, and where religious preachers and other dark men thunder against Danish society. Denmark is a free and democratic society, and if you do not like it, you actually have the freedom to leave the country. Nobody's holding on to these people. The government is therefore in dialogue with the Danish Parliament's parties on how to make more effective efforts against radical religious preachers and anti-democratic religions. We're gonna have to, among other things. look at the possibilities of depriving religious preachers of public recognition, and we also need to look at what we can do to prevent hate preachers from coming here, and we need to criminalise statements that are incompatible with Danish legislation. There is nothing we are afraid to look at, and there is nothing we are afraid to investigate, and that is why we must also give ourselves time and opportunity to discuss this between the parties. The government wants us to recognise Denmark in the future too for the things we uphold: freedom of expression, democracy, equality and responsibility for the community, and we need to make it quite clear that when you come to Denmark, you have just come to a country built on a foundation of freedoms, a foundation that generations before us have laid, precisely a foundation of freedom of religion, of freedom of expression; you can say what you want, you can draw what you want, you can have complete equality. And on top of that foundation, you can build your own life. You just can't break the foundation, and we have to be extremely precise about that. The government is therefore pursuing a consistent immigration policy and will ensure better integration, where far fewer refugees and immigrants are outside the labour market and where fewer live in parallel societies. But that also means that we must have the will to do so. Against this background, I welcome this question and, as I said in my introduction, I think it is extremely relevant. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20160503183012	Thank you. The history of Denmark in recent decades has taught us that the number of foreigners coming to Denmark is important for our ability to maintain Danish culture, our democratic principles and to finance our welfare societies. These are expensive experiences which we cannot allow to be ignored as well. Against this background, we think it is sensible that Denmark's Statistics make these projections of the population composition, and we think it is good that the Danish People's Party is taking the initiative to have a good debate on how the different parties view the figures. The figures in themselves can be completely indifferent if it was not because the new citizens are hitting hard in a series of boring statistics. Men with a non-Western background appear 2.5 times as often in crime statistics as others, only half of the same men are in employment, while three out of four in the rest of the population, and we note that we have not managed to overcome the parallel societies that have arisen in Denmark. Although we can also track progress, for example. with regard to the level of education for young women of ethnic origin other than Danish and the number of children who attend day care institutions, we cannot escape the fact that the record high level of asylum for 2015 and the resulting family reunifications will be a huge challenge for Danish society, especially for the lowest-paid and socially most vulnerable Danes. We know that it is they who will in practice bear the burden of integration both in the labour market and in the residential areas. The increasing number of refugees also means an increased need for the expansion of the public sector. It will require 5,000 new homes, 300 new reception classes, 80 new day care institutions and 1,200 new educators alone to maintain the current level of welfare according to KL. Do we even believe in this at a time of severe cuts in the municipal economy? And who will be the losers when the quality of the welfare state fails? Right, all those who can't pay for private schools, kindergarten and hospitals themselves. The Socialists have a historic obligation to all those who are not born with a silver spoon in the butt, and we will not sit and watch that migration undermines the welfare state. That is why we in the Socialist Group have supported a strict policy of asylum and family reunification. This is also reflected in our proposal for adoption. We cannot continue with an influx that we saw in 2015. Of course, we could choose to abandon the ambition of integrating the many refugees and family members who come in these years, as we hear from the Danish People's Party. We could have people living permanently in camps on Danish soil until they could be sent home. In this way the labour market does not have to open up, housing should not be indicated, the reception classes should not be set up. All we had to do was travel some tents and some barracks and send some food and some medicine and blankets into the camps, and that would allow us to house many more and we would avoid the problems of integration. But we do not support this on the part of the Socialists. We are in favour of integration, partly because it is uncertain when it will be possible for them to return home for years, partly because we will not see children growing up on Danish soil without the possibility of being able to function in our society. And what do we do on the day a family knocks on the door of the hospital and asks if their children can be operated on for a serious illness? I find it hard to imagine that the Danes believe that we should slam the door in the head of such a family. By integration, I do not mean that everyone should be the same. We do not want to ban scarves in public spaces or restrict shawarma on Nørrebrogade, but we want to make it quite clear that there are parts of the foreign culture that are carried to Denmark, which we are fervently opposed to and which we want to fight with everything we own. Our movement has been through a hundred years of political struggle for women's equality. Much of the struggle has been fought with religious forces within the Christian Church, which wanted to taboo the female body, restrict women's access to the labour market and create legal primacy for the man in a marriage. We do not intend to take a single step back in that fight, even if it may be practical with Sharia council or arranged marriages or sex - divided swimming pools. The same applies to democratic principles. We do not believe that the rule of law and the rule of the people can survive if an increasing proportion of the population believes that the Koran is above the constitution. It's no good, even if it's a minority. We need a political showdown of religious fundamentalism and social control, and that showdown cannot be taken once and for all. I think it will be a condition as long as there is immigration to Denmark from non-western countries. However, those parties which continue to support the 1973 immigration freeze and the subsequent tight immigration legislation are also obliged, in our view, to use resources to help the poor and failed states both with emergency relief in the neighbourhoods for war zones, but also with the long-term help to build stable states, infrastructure and education for the next generation. Therefore, we were not in favour of the cuts in Danish foreign funds for 2016 made by the current government, which is why we see the need for urgent spending of 200 million. kr. We have already proposed and set out funding for more help in the neighbourhood. The Social Democrats' proposal for adoption, on which we will vote on another day, shows all these considerations in a short overall text which we look forward to discussing with the other parties of the Folketing.

The Folketing wants all citizens in Denmark to respect Danish culture, Danish traditions, fundamental democratic principles, freedoms and gender equality. Against this background, the Danish Parliament notes that initiatives must be taken to combat anti-democratic and extremist environments and repressive social control. Parliament therefore calls on the government to ensure that non-western immigration is restricted so that the necessary integration can be achieved. Parliament calls on the government to provide the necessary assistance to the refugees in the neighbourhood. • V 50).

It is not crucial for Enhedslisten whether people or their parents were born in Denmark or in non-western countries. Some of the participants in recent years' debate about what the questioners call the composition of the population formulate themselves in a way that gives you the impression that they see a contradiction between being Danish and being Muslim or perhaps even between being Danish and having dark skin or dark hair. Too many times the debate hears the concept of Danes opposed to the concept of Muslims. But it is for Enhedslisten a matter of course that we in Denmark have the freedom to belong to all sorts of Christians, Muslim, Jewish or other religions or not to believe in anything at all, at least not of extraterrestrial beings. No, it's not crucial where people or their parents are born. It is essential, however, that we are able to integrate the people who are granted a residence permit in Denmark, whether they come here to seek protection from war or persecution, or to live with their family or to work. When the narrow right-wing majority of the Folketing decides that newcomers should only receive public benefits at a level equivalent to half of the benefits received by the recipients of cash benefits, this is to the detriment of integration. It is because it separates refugees as a special group of poor people who get a bad start to life in Denmark. They can only afford the worst housing and the cheapest food, and they, and especially their children, cannot afford to pay a quota to sports clubs and other parts of the famous Danish association life, which has previously proved so suitable for integrating people coming from outside. If the separation of refugees as a special group of poor at the bottom of society is carried out as consistently as the right wing suggests, there is indeed reason to fear for the composition and cohesion of the population, because then the right wing succeeds in cementing a them and our society. Another cause for concern is the trend towards an increasing number of people living their lives in Denmark without Danish citizenship. In this way they live in a certain sense outside society because they do not have the civil rights, the right to vote and the freedom of travel enjoyed by the vast majority of the population. Monday morning pointed out recently, there were 2% in 1979. of the resident population who did not have Danish citizenship. Today the figure is 8%. That figure will grow if the right and the Social Democrats maintain the very strict rules on obtaining Danish citizenship, as they agreed when they concluded the latest citizenship agreement in October 2015. Denmark cannot withdraw from the world. Some immigration will take place up to 2050 because of war and oppression, because of globalisation and the open borders of the European Union and because of employers' need for skilled labour. We can only have a limited impact on the number of immigrants and refugees. But what we have full control of as politicians in the Danish Parliament is how we receive the people who come here and whether we give them proper integration for their own benefit and not least for the benefit of all of us who live here in Denmark. Only in this way can we do something good for the composition and cohesion of the population. Then I must say that we in the Enhedslist support the proposal for adoption that the radical rapporteur will be putting forward on our behalf here a little later.

Denmark must, of course, help the persecuted and the people in need, but this must be done primarily in the neighbourhoods. If we get this second approach, the number will regulate by itself. So we do not need to discuss it with the number any longer, because then we will have a policy that will ensure that we only get those who contribute and that those who cannot find out will have to return home. Let me be quite clear: Denmark needs foreigners. There are many Europeans, 100,000, who work in Denmark. We should be glad to say thank you to them. There are many people from the rest of the world who contribute to the success of Danish companies and to Denmark's prosperity. We should be happy about that. The Danish People's Party has asked a question about the consequences of immigration. This is a good opportunity to talk about the fact that there is both something called refugees. We must help them primarily in the neighbourhoods, and we would also like to take some UN refugees, with whom Denmark can match itself in the camps down there, and then there is immigration, which must take place in such a way that people are self-supporting, do not commit crime and do not work against the Danish society. Of course, the whole world cannot live in Denmark, we can quickly agree on that. But Denmark must be an open society. That is what has laid the foundations for Denmark's wealth. We should not close ourselves or open a barn gate, but we should attract the right ones, so we will have a better Denmark and a richer Denmark. The Liberal Alliance makes its own proposal for adoption, which I would like to read out: Proposals for adoption The Danish Parliament notes that a consistent immigration policy must continue to be pursued so that people who are granted a residence permit in Denmark are self-supporting and law-abiding and respect for the rule of the people and the basic freedoms on which the country is built. Against this background, the Danish Parliament calls on the government to work towards a reduction in the part of immigration that does not contribute positively to Danish society, and to initiate initiatives that help to combat anti-democratic and extremist environments. The Danish Parliament wants Denmark's contribution to dealing with humanitarian crises as a result of e.g.: war and distress are given as help in the neighbourhoods where efforts are most useful, and not as today are targeted the reception and handling of a very large number of asylum seekers in Denmark. • V 51).
