ParlaMint-DK_20160503201333	Today a debate has been called for, based on the growing number of immigrants. I understand from the text of the question that those who have raised the question are concerned about the cohesion of Danish society in the light of this population growth. But in the alternative, we disagree that cohesion presupposes as homogeneous and uniform a population as possible. We disagree that cohesion is threatened by diversity. Diversity and, above all, the ability to accommodate and exploit forces in this way are a key part of society's cohesion. This certainly applies to the cohesion on which we believe a rich and good society should be built. Coherence isn't about being the same. It's about trusting and being curious, it's about learning from each other. The context of Danish society is based, in my best opinion, on open mind, openness, equality, tolerance and trust, as well as a welfare system that takes care of those who need help. It is based on giving each other freedom and opportunities to create the life we want. It is based on liberal freedoms and social equality ideals, and then it is based on humanistic values of equal value and equal right to citizenship. So it is not values that are peculiar to Danes who were born with two Danish parents and grew up with Dannebrog in the garden. These are values that people all over the globe can possess and have grown up with or wanted to live out. You can be German, French, Indian, Afghan, etc. and at the same time have these values. That is why I am not always so convinced that we are doing anyone a favour by calling them Danish values. These are values that we subscribe to in Denmark and that we must safeguard. But it is not the values you necessarily live out because you are a Dane, and certainly not the values you can be expected not to possess, just because your parents are not Danish. When you hear the debates on integration and aid for those people who are currently fleeing civil war and terrorist movements, you can be left with the impression that cohesion is a scarce resource that we need to protect. The more people who want to be involved, the less cohesion power there is for all of us. But it's not like that. Coherence is not a static size. It's something we create together every single day. We create it by seeing each other as resources and not as competitors. Over the last few months, we have witnessed the adoption of legislative proposals and symbolic policy measures that have disregarded quite fundamental values on which Danish society is founded, in an attempt to let people know that they are not welcome. We have created the idea that as one of the richest countries in the world we cannot afford to help people in need, that it will overturn our entire welfare system and reduce our well-being. But if something threatens our cohesion, it is precisely the attempt to play out citizens against each other and create scare pictures. It is precisely the way we talk about and treat each other that challenges the cohesion force, not the number of people. I agree that our cohesion is based on fundamental values and fundamental rights, which we must uphold. This is so much about freedoms. It is precisely they who ensure that everyone can thrive together. It is quite true that we must stand by these values and rights. They must not be bent down, but that also means that we must be consistent in our defences and not be selective according to how it fits into our own political agendas. I am not concerned that increased population growth will affect our identity. But I can be concerned that we are already in the process of letting go of some of the values that characterise us. So, although I do not share the premise of this question debate, I certainly think it is appropriate to consider which society we go and build together every day and which values we will build on and build on. Of course, we can include the people who come here for the next decades, but it is clear that we need to be better at integrating people into the labour market, into civil society and into society. Here we need to think in new ways, be creative and create new opportunities across society. Most important of all for successful integration is that people contribute to the society in which they live and that they feel a shared ownership. We must be much better at strengthening the enormous potential of civil society, which has been demonstrated in recent years, when a great many people have stood ready to take people on the run and help them to find their way into their new communities. A prerequisite for success is that we stop telling each other that Denmark is too small a country to accommodate diversity. We must have the courage to let go of that story and instead be united in creating a new one based on the potential of the individual and with a mutual desire to learn from each other.

ParlaMint-DK_20160503205020	Thank you. Denmark is under pressure. There is no doubt about that in a world where increasing inequality and climate problems have led to more wars and conflicts. I've experienced it myself. I come from a municipality where we have had several vulnerable residential areas, where I myself, when I was younger, and also others I know, have not dared to go through the residential areas because one has known how much happened in there: Little brothers have been recruited by big brothers, child guards are walking around with bulletproof vests, and I have seen police cars patrolling constantly. These are vulnerable residential areas located right here in our country. This is the result of the lack of focus on housing policy by changing governments. It is also clear, especially in recent times, that in some of these parallel societies there is a large increase in social control, with almost parallel legal communities. So I would say to the Danish People's Party that our idea of how to include this huge population growth in Danish society is simply that fewer parallel societies need to be created. This is not done by halving the benefits of refugees so that they can only afford to live in vulnerable residential areas. This is not done by introducing a ceiling on cash benefits, so the socially disadvantaged will have to move into the ghetto regions. On the contrary, you create more ghettos that way. That's what you do by building more cheap homes. Together we could reduce the basic capital so that the municipalities could afford to build more homes. This is done by ensuring combined and flexible rental so that you can mix the masses of the population so that we don't get these parallel societies. This is done with better opportunities for urban renewal and demolition. We have together, sir. Martin Henriksen and I, visited, I think it was the Gellerup Park, where something was actually torn down to build something new. It was something you had to start, and then you do it by making sure that at least 25%. of general housing is part of the new building. This is something that the Danish People's Party is changing. It will also lead to more parallel societies. As far as supporting democratic forces is concerned, the focus is on anti-radicalisation, but the focus should also be on combating social control of more shelters, more retirement homes with after-protection and with municipalities that have a clear readiness to help the people who have been under social control out of the parallel society in which they exist. So I'm not saying any of this is gonna be easy. I do not think that it is just easy to get the large amount of people coming to the country right now, included and integrated into our society, but I am sure that a good step on the way will be to take a hard line against the parallel societies that are, and not create more of them, and to help people break out of social control. There are 50 million refugees in the world at the moment, so we have to intervene and resolve those conflicts, solve the problems of the wars that are in the world if we are to stop the flow of refugees. I think this is a very good way to solve it instead of just closing borders where they can then stand there and be killed.

ParlaMint-DK_20160503213252	Thank you. I just want to thank you for the debate, thank you to the parties and the Minister, and thank you, in particular, to Conservatives, the Left and the Socialists, for recognising the problems and challenges associated with extreme immigration, especially the non-western part of immigration. If we do not stop development, Denmark will change its character decisively, and we do not want that. The Danish People's Party wants to safeguard our cultural identity, and we believe that this requires more tightening of asylum and immigration policy. At this stage, it does not seem that a majority can be reached for a single vote, but I think it is good, after all, that there are four parties that have today expressed a desire to reduce non-western immigration. We intend to follow this up by the Danish People's Party. When we from the Danish People's Party in 2010 proposed precisely this, that is to say to reduce non-western immigration, no other party thought it was a good idea. Now I understand that there are at least three other parties that think that this is quite good. So I'd like to sign for that. It will be interesting to see what can come of it in the slightly longer term. However, I would like to thank all parties for the debate and the discussion. I think this has been a good discussion.

I think that by far the majority of people who come to this country would like to be independent of public funds and would like to be allowed to do something if there were a job. But there's no work!
